</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the value of the expression \( x_I x_D + y_I y_D + x_E x_M + y_E y_M \) given the geometric configuration of the triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with incenter \( I \), circumcircle \( \Gamma \), point \( D \) on the major arc \( BC \) such that \( DI \perp BC \), point \( E \) as the intersection of \( DA \) and \( BC \), and \( M \) as the midpoint of the minor arc \( BC \).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Incenter and Coordinates**:
   - The incenter \( I \) is the point where the angle bisectors of \( \triangle ABC \) intersect. However, we do not need specific coordinates for \( I \) to solve the problem.

2. **Point \( D \)**:
   - \( D \) is on the major arc \( BC \) such that \( DI \perp BC \). Since \( BC \) is horizontal (we can assume this without loss of generality), \( D \) must lie vertically above or below \( I \). Therefore, the x-coordinates of \( D \) and \( I \) are the same, i.e., \( x_D = x_I \).

3. **Midpoint of Arc \( BC \)**:
   - \( M \) is the midpoint of the minor arc \( BC \). This means \( M \) lies on the circumcircle and is equidistant from \( B \) and \( C \).

4. **Intersection Point \( E \)**:
   - \( E \) is the intersection of \( DA \) and \( BC \). Since \( BC \) is horizontal, \( E \) lies on the x-axis, so \( y_E = 0 \).

5. **Expression Simplification**:
   - The expression to evaluate is \( x_I x_D + y_I y_D + x_E x_M + y_E y_M \).
   - Since \( x_D = x_I \), the term \( x_I x_D \) simplifies to \( x_I^2 \).
   - The term \( y_E y_M \) is zero because \( y_E = 0 \).

   Therefore, the expression simplifies to:
   \[
   x_I^2 + y_I y_D + x_E x_M
   \]

6. **Dot Product Analysis**:
   - The term \( y_I y_D \) involves the y-coordinates of \( I \) and \( D \). Since \( D \) is vertically aligned with \( I \), the y-coordinates of \( I \) and \( D \) are related by the perpendicularity condition.
   - The term \( x_E x_M \) involves the x-coordinates of \( E \) and \( M \). Since \( E \) lies on the x-axis and \( M \) is the midpoint of the minor arc \( BC \), the x-coordinates of \( E \) and \( M \) are related by the symmetry of the circumcircle.

7. **Final Simplification**:
   - Given the geometric properties and the perpendicularity conditions, the terms \( y_I y_D \) and \( x_E x_M \) cancel each other out due to the symmetry and the perpendicularity of \( DI \) to \( BC \).

Thus, the value of the expression \( x_I x_D + y_I y_D + x_E x_M + y_E y_M \) is:

./sepal_ui/message/__init__.py<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from typing import Optional


def create_meshgrid(x: 'torch.Tensor', normalized_coordinates: 'Optional[bool]'
    ) ->torch.Tensor:
    assert len(x.shape) == 4, x.shape
    _, _, height, width = x.shape
    _device, _dtype = x.device, x.dtype
    if normalized_coordinates:
        xs = torch.linspace(-1.0, 1.0, width, device=_device, dtype=_dtype)
        ys = torch.linspace(-1.0, 1.0, height, device=_device, dtype=_dtype)
    else:
        xs = torch.linspace(0, width - 1, width, device=_device, dtype=_dtype)
        ys = torch.linspace(0, height - 1, height, device=_device, dtype=_dtype
            )
    return torch.meshgrid(ys, xs)


class SoftArgmax2D(nn.Module):
    """Creates a module that computes the Spatial Soft-Argmax 2D
    of a given input heatmap.

    Returns the index of the maximum 2d coordinates of the give map.
    The output order is x-coord and y-coord.

    Arguments:
        normalized_coordinates (Optional[bool]): wether to return the
          coordinates normalized in the range of [-1, 1]. Otherwise,
          it will return the coordinates in the range of the input shape.
          Default is True.

    Shape:
        - Input: :math:`(B, N, H, W)`
        - Output: :math:`(B, N, 2)`

    Examples::
        >>> input = torch.rand(1, 4, 2, 3)
        >>> m = tgm.losses.SpatialSoftArgmax2d()
        >>> coords = m(input)  # 1x4x2
        >>> x_coord, y_coord = torch.chunk(coords, dim=-1, chunks=2)
    """

    def __init__(self, normalized_coordinates: 'Optional[bool]'=True) ->None:
        super(SoftArgmax2D, self).__init__()
        self.normalized_coordinates: 'Optional[bool]' = normalized_coordinates
        self.eps: 'float' = 1e-06

    def forward(self, input: 'torch.Tensor') ->torch.Tensor:
        if not torch.is_tensor(input):
            raise TypeError('Input input type is not a torch.Tensor. Got {}'
                .format(type(input)))
        if not len(input.shape) == 4:
            raise ValueError('Invalid input shape, we expect BxCxHxW. Got: {}'
                .format(input.shape))
        batch_size, channels, _height, _width = input.shape
        x: 'torch.Tensor' = input.view(batch_size, channels, -1)
        exp_x = torch.exp(x - torch.max(x, dim=-1, keepdim=True)[0])
        exp_x_sum = 1.0 / (exp_x.sum(dim=-1, keepdim=True) + self.eps)
        pos_y, pos_x = create_meshgrid(input, self.normalized_coordinates)
        pos_x = pos_x.reshape(-1)
        pos_y = pos_y.reshape(-1)
        expected_y: 'torch.Tensor' = torch.sum(pos_y * exp_x * exp_x_sum,
            dim=-1, keepdim=True)
        expected_x: 'torch.Tensor' = torch.sum(pos_x * exp_x * exp_x_sum,
            dim=-1, keepdim=True)
        output: 'torch.Tensor' = torch.cat([expected_x, expected_y], dim=-1)
        return output.view(batch_size, channels, 2)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
<#sep#>Let \(ABCD-EFGH\) be a unit cube. \(I\) is the midpoint of \(BF\), \(J\) is the midpoint of \(BC\), and \(K\) is the midpoint of \(IJ\). Find the shortest distance between the line segments \(EC\) and \(HK\).

<think>
Okay, let's see. I need to find the shortest distance between the line segments EC and HK in a unit cube ABCD-EFGH. Hmm. First, I should probably visualize the cube and label all the points correctly. Let me recall that in a unit cube, all edges are 1 unit long. 

The cube is labeled ABCD-EFGH. So, typically, ABCD is the bottom face, and EFGH is the top face, with A connected to E, B to F, etc. Let me confirm that. So, if ABCD is the bottom square, then E is above A, F above B, G above C, and H above D. Right.

Now, points I, J, K are defined as midpoints. I is the midpoint of BF. Since BF is an edge connecting B to F. Since it's a unit cube, BF has length 1, so the midpoint I would be halfway along that edge. Similarly, J is the midpoint of BC. BC is an edge on the bottom face from B to C, so its midpoint J is halfway there. Then K is the midpoint of IJ. So first, maybe I need to find the coordinates of all these points to make things clearer.

Let me assign coordinates to the cube. Let's place point A at the origin (0,0,0). Then, since it's a unit cube, the coordinates would be:

- A: (0,0,0)
- B: (1,0,0) [right next to A on the x-axis]
- C: (1,1,0) [next to B on the y-axis]
- D: (0,1,0)
- E: (0,0,1) [above A]
- F: (1,0,1) [above B]
- G: (1,1,1) [above C]
- H: (0,1,1) [above D]

Okay, that makes sense. Now, let's find the coordinates of points I, J, K.

Point I is the midpoint of BF. B is (1,0,0) and F is (1,0,1). Midpoint formula: average each coordinate. So I = ((1+1)/2, (0+0)/2, (0+1)/2) = (1, 0, 0.5).

Point J is the midpoint of BC. B is (1,0,0), C is (1,1,0). Midpoint J = ((1+1)/2, (0+1)/2, (0+0)/2) = (1, 0.5, 0).

Now, point K is the midpoint of IJ. I is (1, 0, 0.5) and J is (1, 0.5, 0). So midpoint K = ((1+1)/2, (0 + 0.5)/2, (0.5 + 0)/2) = (1, 0.25, 0.25).

So K is at (1, 0.25, 0.25). Got that.

Now, we need to find the shortest distance between the line segments EC and HK.

First, let's figure out the coordinates of E, C, H, and K again.

E is (0,0,1), C is (1,1,0). So line segment EC connects (0,0,1) to (1,1,0).

H is (0,1,1), and K is (1, 0.25, 0.25). So line segment HK connects (0,1,1) to (1, 0.25, 0.25).

We need the shortest distance between these two line segments in 3D space.

I remember that the shortest distance between two skew lines can be found using the formula involving the cross product of their direction vectors and the vector connecting a point on each line. But first, I need to confirm if the lines EC and HK are skew, parallel, intersecting, etc.

First, let's check if they intersect. If they do, the distance would be zero. If they are parallel, the distance can be computed using another method. If they are skew, then we use the cross product method.

Let me parametrize both lines.

For line EC: Let's write parametric equations.

Point E is (0,0,1), and point C is (1,1,0). The direction vector of EC is C - E = (1-0, 1-0, 0-1) = (1,1,-1). So parametric equations can be written as:

E + t*(1,1,-1) where t ∈ [0,1].

So:

x = 0 + t*1 = t

y = 0 + t*1 = t

z = 1 + t*(-1) = 1 - t

Similarly, for line HK: H is (0,1,1), K is (1, 0.25, 0.25). Direction vector is K - H = (1-0, 0.25 -1, 0.25 -1) = (1, -0.75, -0.75). So parametric equations for HK can be written as H + s*(1, -0.75, -0.75), where s ∈ [0,1].

So:

x = 0 + s*1 = s

y = 1 + s*(-0.75) = 1 - 0.75s

z = 1 + s*(-0.75) = 1 - 0.75s

Now, check if the lines intersect. To check for intersection, we can set the parametric equations equal and see if there are t and s in [0,1] that satisfy all three equations.

Set t = s (since x-coordinates would need to be equal: t = s)

Then:

x: t = s

y: t = 1 - 0.75s

But since s = t, substitute:

t = 1 - 0.75t

t + 0.75t = 1

1.75t = 1

t = 1 / 1.75 = 4/7 ≈ 0.571, which is within [0,1]

Check z-coordinate:

For EC: z = 1 - t = 1 - 4/7 = 3/7 ≈ 0.428

For HK: z = 1 - 0.75s = 1 - 0.75*(4/7) = 1 - 3/7 = 4/7 ≈ 0.571

But 3/7 ≠ 4/7, so the z-coordinates don't match. Therefore, the lines do not intersect. Hence, they are skew lines.

Therefore, the shortest distance between them can be computed using the formula for the distance between two skew lines.

The formula is: |(b - a) · (v × w)| / |v × w|, where a and b are points on the two lines, and v and w are their direction vectors.

Alternatively, sometimes expressed as |(PQ) · (v × w)| / |v × w| where PQ is the vector connecting points P and Q on the two lines.

So, let's apply that.

First, let's define:

Line EC: point P = E = (0,0,1), direction vector v = (1,1,-1)

Line HK: point Q = H = (0,1,1), direction vector w = (1, -0.75, -0.75)

Compute vector PQ = Q - P = (0 - 0, 1 - 0, 1 - 1) = (0,1,0)

Wait, Q is H (0,1,1) and P is E (0,0,1), so PQ is (0,1,1) - (0,0,1) = (0,1,0). Right.

Then compute v × w (cross product of direction vectors):

v = (1,1,-1)

w = (1, -0.75, -0.75)

Compute cross product:

|i    j      k|
|1    1     -1|
|1 -0.75 -0.75|

import torch.nn as nn


class CustomBatchNormManualFunction(torch.autograd.Function):
    """
    This torch.autograd.Function implements a functional custom version of the batch norm operation for MLPs.
    Using torch.autograd.Function allows you to write a custom backward function.
    The function will be called from the nn.Module CustomBatchNormManualModule
    Inside forward the tensors are (automatically) not recorded for automatic differentiation since the backward
    pass is done via the backward method.
    The forward pass is not called directly but via the apply() method. This makes sure that the context objects
    are dealt with correctly. Example:
      my_bn_fct = CustomBatchNormManualFunction()
      normalized = fct.apply(input, gamma, beta, eps)
    """

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, gamma, beta, eps=1e-05):
        """
        Compute the batch normalization
        
        Args:
          ctx: context object handling storing and retrival of tensors and constants and specifying
               whether tensors need gradients in backward pass
          input: input tensor of shape (n_batch, n_neurons)
          gamma: variance scaling tensor, applied per neuron, shpae (n_neurons)
          beta: mean bias tensor, applied per neuron, shpae (n_neurons)
          eps: small float added to the variance for stability
        Returns:
          out: batch-normalized tensor
    
        TODO:
          Implement the forward pass of batch normalization
          Store constant non-tensor objects via ctx.constant=myconstant
          Store tensors which you need in the backward pass via ctx.save_for_backward(tensor1, tensor2, ...)
          Intermediate results can be decided to be either recomputed in the backward pass or to be stored
          for the backward pass. Do not store tensors which are unnecessary for the backward pass to save memory!
          For the case that you make use of torch.var be aware that the flag unbiased=False should be set.
        """
        x = input
        mu = x.mean(dim=0)
        std = x.std(dim=0, unbiased=False)
        var = torch.sqrt(std * std + eps)
        x_centered = x - mu
        x_hat = x_centered / var
        out = gamma * x_hat + beta
        ctx.save_for_backward(x, mu, std, var, gamma)
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        """
        Compute backward pass of the batch normalization.
        
        Args:
          ctx: context object handling storing and retrival of tensors and constants and specifying
               whether tensors need gradients in backward pass
        Returns:
          out: tuple containing gradients for all input arguments
        
        TODO:
          Retrieve saved tensors and constants via ctx.saved_tensors and ctx.constant
          Compute gradients for inputs where ctx.needs_input_grad[idx] is True. Set gradients for other
          inputs to None. This should be decided dynamically.
        """
        x, mu, _std, var, gamma = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_gamma, grad_beta = None, None, None
        x_hat = (x - mu) / var
        if ctx.needs_input_grad[2]:
            grad_beta = grad_output.sum(0)
        if ctx.needs_input_grad[1]:
            grad_gamma = (grad_output * x_hat).sum(0)
        if ctx.needs_input_grad[0]:
            c1 = grad_output.mean(dim=0)
            c2 = (grad_output * x_hat).mean(dim=0)
            grad_input = gamma / var * (grad_output - c1 - x_hat * c2)
        return grad_input, grad_gamma, grad_beta, None


class CustomBatchNormManualModule(nn.Module):
    """
    This nn.module implements a custom version of the batch norm operation for MLPs.
    In self.forward the functional version CustomBatchNormManualFunction.forward is called.
    The automatic differentiation of PyTorch calls the backward method of this function in the backward pass.
    """

    def __init__(self, n_neurons, eps=1e-05):
        """
        Initializes CustomBatchNormManualModule object.
        
        Args:
          n_neurons: int specifying the number of neurons
          eps: small float to be added to the variance for stability
        
        TODO:
          Save parameters for the number of neurons and eps.
          Initialize parameters gamma and beta via nn.Parameter
        """
        super(CustomBatchNormManualModule, self).__init__()
        self.n_neurons = n_neurons
        self.eps = eps
        self.gammas = torch.nn.Parameter(torch.ones(n_neurons))
        self.betas = torch.nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(n_neurons))

    def forward(self, input):
        """
        Compute the batch normalization via CustomBatchNormManualFunction
        
        Args:
          input: input tensor of shape (n_batch, n_neurons)
        Returns:
          out: batch-normalized tensor
        
        TODO:
          Check for the correctness of the shape of the input tensor.
          Instantiate a CustomBatchNormManualFunction.
          Call it via its .apply() method.
        """
        shape = input.size()
        if len(shape) == 1:
            input.view(1, -1)
            shape = input.size()
        if len(shape) > 2:
            raise ValueError('Expected 1-D or 2-D tensor (got {})'.format(
                str(shape)))
        elif input.shape[1] != self.n_neurons:
            raise ValueError('Expected _ x {} tensor (got {} x {})'.format(
                str(self.n_neurons), str(shape[0]), str(shape[1])))
        fct = CustomBatchNormManualFunction()
        out = fct.apply(input, self.gammas, self.betas, self.eps)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'n_neurons': 4}]

Compute cross product:

|i    j      k|
|1    1     -1|
|1 -0.75 -0.75|

= i*(1*(-0.75) - (-1)*(-0.75)) - j*(1*(-0.75) - (-1)*1) + k*(1*(-0.75) - 1*1)

Calculate each component:

i-component: 1*(-0.75) - (-1)*(-0.75) = -0.75 - 0.75 = -1.5

j-component: -(1*(-0.75) - (-1)*1) = -(-0.75 + 1) = -(0.25) = -0.25

k-component: 1*(-0.75) - 1*1 = -0.75 - 1 = -1.75

So v × w = (-1.5, -0.25, -1.75)

v × w = (v2w3 - v3w2, v3w1 - v1w3, v1w2 - v2w1)

So:

First component (i): v_y*w_z - v_z*w_y = 1*(-0.75) - (-1)*(-0.75) = -0.75 - 0.75 = -1.5

Second component (j): v_z*w_x - v_x*w_z = (-1)*1 - 1*(-0.75) = -1 + 0.75 = -0.25. But since the j-component is subtracted, it becomes -(-0.25) = 0.25? Wait, no, wait. The cross product formula is:

So for the j-component, which is the second component, it's v3w1 - v1w3. Then in the determinant, when expanding, the j component is negative of that.

Given v = (v1, v2, v3) and w = (w1, w2, w3):

v × w = (v2w3 - v3w2, v3w1 - v1w3, v1w2 - v2w1)

So:

i component: v2w3 - v3w2 = 1*(-0.75) - (-1)*(-0.75) = -0.75 - 0.75 = -1.5

j component: v3w1 - v1w3 = (-1)*1 - 1*(-0.75) = -1 + 0.75 = -0.25

But wait, in the standard cross product, the j component is actually the negative of that? Wait, no. Let me check the cross product definition again. The cross product determinant:

i  j  k
v1 v2 v3
w1 w2 w3

So expanding, it's i*(v2w3 - v3w2) - j*(v1w3 - v3w1) + k*(v1w2 - v2w1). So the j component is - (v1w3 - v3w1) = v3w1 - v1w3. So my previous calculation is correct. So j component is -0.25.

Similarly, k component: v1w2 - v2w1 = 1*(-0.75) - 1*1 = -0.75 - 1 = -1.75.

Hence, v × w = (-1.5, -0.25, -1.75). Correct.

Dot product:

0*(-1.5) + 1*(-0.25) + 0*(-1.75) = 0 - 0.25 + 0 = -0.25

The absolute value is | -0.25 | = 0.25

So the key point is the squares form a connected region on their own. 

Thus, the segments can have holes, but they are still contiguous. Thus a segment is a maximal or any subset of connected squares (allowing holes in their own), but any segment must be a single connected component.

Thus, a "contiguous segment" must be a single connected region (allowing holes in the overall shape, but the segment itself is a connected region of the squares).

Therefore, when rearranging, the segments must be moved as whole connected regions, but they can have holes.

Putting this all aside, how do I approach this computationally?

Well, given the problem is likely for programming competition and considering the input examples are small, the following approach can be considered:

- The minimal required number is the minimal number such that the first shape can be partitioned into regions (contiguous), each of which can be rotated or reflected to fit into the second shape, and those regions can be arranged without overlapping in the second shape.

- The algorithm can be:

1. Enumerate all possible partitions of the first shape into k contiguous regions. For each possible such partition, check if each region can be arranged (rotated/reflected) to fit into the second shape. 

2. The minimal such k is the answer.

But clearly, this brute-force approach is computationally not feasible for any non-trivial problem.

So, perhaps there's an approach where for each region in the first shape, check all possible rotations and reflections to see if they can form parts of the second shape, and track the coverage. 

Alternatively, maybe it's better to realize that the minimal number is equal to the minimum k over all possible tilings where the outline of the pieces in the first and rearranged tiles in the second align properly.

import torch.utils.data
import torch.utils.data.distributed
import torch.nn.init
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def Normalizations(tensor_size=None, normalization=None, available=False,
    **kwargs):
    """Does normalization on 4D tensor.

    Args:
        tensor_size: shape of tensor in BCHW
            (None/any integer >0, channels, height, width)
        normalization: None/batch/group/instance/layer/pixelwise
        available: if True, returns all available normalization methods
        groups: for group (GroupNorm), when not provided groups is the center
            value of all possible - ex: for a tensor_size[1] = 128, groups is
            set to 16 as the possible groups are [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128]
        affine: for group and instance normalization, default False
        elementwise_affine: for layer normalization. default True
    """
    list_available = ['batch', 'group', 'instance', 'layer', 'pixelwise']
    if available:
        return list_available
    normalization = normalization.lower()
    assert normalization in list_available, 'Normalization must be None/' + '/'.join(
        list_available)
    if normalization == 'batch':
        return torch.nn.BatchNorm2d(tensor_size[1])
    elif normalization == 'group':
        affine = kwargs['affine'] if 'affine' in kwargs.keys() else False
        if 'groups' in kwargs.keys():
            return torch.nn.GroupNorm(kwargs['groups'], tensor_size[1],
                affine=affine)
        else:
            possible = [(tensor_size[1] // i) for i in range(tensor_size[1],
                0, -1) if tensor_size[1] % i == 0]
            groups = possible[len(possible) // 2]
            return torch.nn.GroupNorm(groups, tensor_size[1], affine=affine)
    elif normalization == 'instance':
        affine = kwargs['affine'] if 'affine' in kwargs.keys() else False
        return torch.nn.InstanceNorm2d(tensor_size[1], affine=affine)
    elif normalization == 'layer':
        elementwise_affine = kwargs['elementwise_affine'
            ] if 'elementwise_affine' in kwargs.keys() else True
        return torch.nn.LayerNorm(tensor_size[1:], elementwise_affine=
            elementwise_affine)
    elif normalization == 'pixelwise':
        return PixelWise()


class Activations(nn.Module):
    """ All the usual activations along with maxout, relu + maxout and swish.
    MaxOut (maxo) - https://arxiv.org/pdf/1302.4389.pdf
    Swish - https://arxiv.org/pdf/1710.05941v1.pdf

    Args:
        activation: relu/relu6/lklu/elu/prelu/tanh/sigm/maxo/rmxo/swish
        channels: parameter for prelu, default is 1
    """

    def __init__(self, activation='relu', channels=1):
        super(Activations, self).__init__()
        if activation is not None:
            activation = activation.lower()
        self.activation = activation
        self.function = None
        if activation in self.available():
            self.function = getattr(self, '_' + activation)
            if activation == 'prelu':
                self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.rand(channels))
        else:
            self.activation = ''

    def forward(self, tensor):
        if self.function is None:
            return tensor
        return self.function(tensor)

    def _relu(self, tensor):
        return F.relu(tensor)

    def _relu6(self, tensor):
        return F.relu6(tensor)

    def _lklu(self, tensor):
        return F.leaky_relu(tensor)

    def _elu(self, tensor):
        return F.elu(tensor)

    def _prelu(self, tensor):
        return F.prelu(tensor, self.weight)

    def _tanh(self, tensor):
        return torch.tanh(tensor)

    def _sigm(self, tensor):
        return torch.sigmoid(tensor)

    def _maxo(self, tensor):
        assert tensor.size(1) % 2 == 0, 'MaxOut: tensor.size(1) must be even'
        return torch.max(*tensor.split(tensor.size(1) // 2, 1))

    def _rmxo(self, tensor):
        return self._maxo(F.relu(tensor))

    def _swish(self, tensor):
        return tensor * torch.sigmoid(tensor)

    def __repr__(self):
        return self.activation

    @staticmethod
    def available():
        return ['relu', 'relu6', 'lklu', 'elu', 'prelu', 'tanh', 'sigm',
            'maxo', 'rmxo', 'swish']


class PixelWise(torch.nn.Module):
    """ Implemented - https://arxiv.org/pdf/1710.10196.pdf """

    def __init__(self, eps=1e-08):
        super(PixelWise, self).__init__()
        self.eps = eps

    def forward(self, tensor):
        return tensor.div(tensor.pow(2).mean(1, True).add(self.eps).pow(0.5))

    def __repr__(self):
        return 'pixelwise'


class ConvolutionTranspose(nn.Module):
    """
        Parameters/Inputs
            tensor_size = (None/any integer >0, channels, height, width)
            filter_size = list(length=2)/tuple(length=2)/integer
            out_channels = return tensor.size(1)
            strides = list(length=2)/tuple(length=2)/integer
            pad = True/False
            activation = "relu"/"relu6"/"lklu"/"tanh"/"sigm"/"maxo"/"rmxo"/"swish"
            dropout = 0.-1.
            normalization = None/"batch"/"group"/"instance"/"layer"/"pixelwise"
            pre_nm = True/False
            groups = 1, ... out_channels
            weight_nm = True/False -- https://arxiv.org/pdf/1602.07868.pdf
            equalized = True/False -- https://arxiv.org/pdf/1710.10196.pdf

            strides = tuple(strides)

            ), 'ConvolutionTranspose -- pad must be boolean'
        assert isinstance(dropout, float

        assert normalization in [None, 'batch', 'group', 'instance',
            'layer', 'pixelwise'
            ], "Convolution's normalization must be None/batch/group/instance/layer/pixelwise"
        assert isinstance(equalized, bool
            ), 'Convolution -- equalized must be boolean'
        self.equalized = equalized
        if activation is not None:
            activation = activation.lower()
        dilation = kwargs['dilation'] if 'dilation' in kwargs.keys() else (1, 1
            )
        padding = (filter_size[0] // 2, filter_size[1] // 2) if pad else (0, 0)

        pre_expansion, pst_expansion = 1, 1
        if activation in ('maxo', 'rmxo'):
            if pre_nm:
                pre_expansion = 2
            if not pre_nm:
                pst_expansion = 2
        if pre_nm:
            if normalization is not None:
                self.Normalization = Normal<#sep#>1/sqrt(86). Therefore, the distance is 1/sqrt(86).

But this is a possible approach, but computationally heavy? It requires for each s (starting from maximal possible) and then checking all possible tiles of that area in the first input's shape as possible candidates.

First, the first's shape must be divided into k= (area/s) non-overlapping congruent (up to rotation/reflect) tiles, all of area s.

- generate all possible rotations and reflections of the tile.

- then check if the first shape can be divided into such tiles (in some orientation each).

- check similarly if the second can be divided into the same tile, orientations allowed.

The steps could be:

Given two input shapes:

1. Compute area s.

   (Since same area, just take one's area.)

2. Check all possible divisors s_candidate = area of the tile (i.e., divisors of area).

   starting from the maximal possible (area itself) down to 1.

3. For each s_candidate:

   a. If (totalArea % s_candidate !=0) → skip (can't divide in such tiles).

   b. Find all contiguous subregions in the first shape with area s_candidate.

       For each such possible region R:

          generate all rotations and reflections of R's shape (to get all possible variations.

          For this configuration, let's call the tile T= R's base.

          Then, check whether the first can be tiled with multiple copies (matched via rotation/reflection) of T.

          Also, check similarly if the second shape can be tiled with copies of T ((rotation etc allowed).

          If any such R leads to both divisions possible → return (area/s_candidate) as the answer.

4. The first s_candidate that this works for is the maximal possible, giving minimal divisions.

Now the key problem is:

How do I efficiently implement steps 3.b? Especially when the area increases.

Generating all possible regions R of a given area is manageable for small grids (up to say 20x20, area 400). But even that, generating all possible subregions with given area could be expensive.

An additional optimization is that the first step can filter out some candidates early. 

For example, if a s_candidate does not have dimensions that allow the second shape's grid to fit multiples of it.

Perhaps in programming competition, the problem is intended to be solved this way with the steps outlined, but using some heuristics or efficient implementation.

Thus, the algorithm steps can be written as follows:

Implement an algorithm that for each possible s_candidate (starting from largest downwards), down to 1:

   if area mod s_candidate !=0 → skip.

   compute all candidate tiles R in first shape:

      a tile R is a contiguous region (connected as per grid adjacency) with area s_candidate.

   for each such tile R:

      generate all transformations (rotations and reflections) of R → possible tiles.

      note: To represent the tile in a canonical form for checking, so that different orientations can be compared.

      Then, find out if the first shape can be tiled with T across transformations.

      similarly for second shape.

      if both first and second can be divided into tiles congruent to T:

          return area/s_candidate as the answer.

If none of the s_candidate works down to 1 → then worst case is area divisions.

Hence this gives the minimal possible.

Now the question is, how do I perform these steps in code?

First, we need to code:

1. Reading the first and second shapes as grids.

2. Generate all possible contiguous regions of area s (for varying s).

This requires finding all contiguous regions (connected components) of exactly s area.

A connected region is a set of squares connected via edges, forming a single polyomino of s squares.

 Additionally, each region can be represented in a canonical form (like normalized coordinates to account for translations), but for checking if another region can be tiled via rotations.

Hmm. For example:

A tile's representation can be stored as a set of offsets relative to its axis. 

For example, given a region R, we can choose the top-left most point as the origin, and record the positions of other cells relative to that point. This gives a canonical form. Rotations/orientations can then be represented by applying rotations to this canonical form.

Thus, for a region, the canonical form would help us represent rotations and reflections for comparison purposes.

Therefore, the algorithm could proceed as follows:

For each candidate tile region R:

   a. Normalize its coordinates to a canonical form (e.g., translate so that the topmost row is at 0, and the leftmost column is at 0).

   b. Generate all its possible rotations and reflections (rotating every possible  degree, which in grid cells can be 90, 180, 270 degrees, and reflections).

   For each transformed version of the canonical form, store the shape's set of offset coordinates relative to its top-left point of the transform.

This way, any tile's possible transformations are captured in various forms, which can be then checked for possibility to tile the shape.

Thus, when checking if a given tile can be used to partition a shape, we can scan the shape's squares, and attempt to cover them with the tile in various orientations.

But even this is computationally expensive for larger shapes, but for small cases like in the examples, it could work.

Another optimization could be:

Once a tile is found that works (can tile both shapes), then that s_candidate is valid, and the minimal number is found (since we're checking from largest to smallest s).

Now, how to check if the entire shape can be partitioned into the given tile (in various orientations)?

This is a tiling problem. How can we do this?

Perhaps a backtracking search: 

Try to place one oriented tile in an empty position of the shape, and recurse on the remaining. 

This is feasible for small s, small area.

Alternatively, use a BFS or some memoization, but for a programming solution, perhaps backtracking can be manageable for small sizes.

Alternatively, it's possible that the first shape is already a single tile (thus s_candidate is the area, so if the tile can be rotated into the second, then output 1.

This case should be checked first.

The core functions needed are:

- For a given region (a list of coordinates), create its canonical form.

- Generate all transformations (rotations and reflections) of a tile's canonical form.

- For a target shape (a grid), check whether it can be tiled with a given set of candidate tile's transformations (rotations and reflections).

Now, the first step to this would be:

Generate all contiguous regions of a particular size in the first shape.

To generate all possible contiguous regions with size s, maybe we can use BFS or DFS-based approach to find all unique polyominoes of that area.

However, this can be computationally intensive for s of, say, 8 squares. For each square in the first shape, start building all possible regions, but with memoization to avoid repeating.

But in any case, let's think of the code.

First, read the two input grids.

Represent each as a grid (for example, a 2D array with 0 or 1, where 1 denotes filled and 0 is empty).

   if area % s_candidate !=0 → continue.

       Generate its canonical form.

    13	class Translator(Box):
    14	    """
    15	    The translator is a Box of Boxes. It reads 2 Json files, the first one being the source language (usually English) and the second one the target language.
    16	    It will replace in the source dictionary every key that exist in both json dictionaries. Following this procedure, every message that is not translated can still be accessed in the source language.
    17	    To access the dictionary keys, instead of using [], you can simply use key name as in an object ex: translator.first_key.secondary_key.
    18	    There are no depth limits, just respect the snake_case convention when naming your keys in the .json files.
    19	
    20	    Args:
    21	        json_folder (str | pathlib.Path): The folder where the dictionaries are stored
    22	        target (str, optional): The language code (IETF BCP 47) of the target lang (it should be the same as the target dictionary). Default to either the language specified in the parameter file or the default one.
    23	
Review the changes and make sure they are as expected. Edit the file again if necessary.

                bias=False, dilation=dilation, groups=groups), name='weight')
        else:

                pre_expansion, out_channels * pst_expansion, filter_size,
                strides, padding, bias=False, groups=groups)
            nn.init.kaiming_normal_(self.ConvolutionTranspose.weight, nn.
                init.calculate_gain('conv2d'))

                gain = kwargs['gain'] if 'gain' in kwargs.keys() else np.sqrt(2
                    )
                fan_in = tensor_size[1] * out_channels * filter_size[0]
                self.scale = gain / np.sqrt(fan_in)
                self.ConvolutionTranspose.weight.data.mul_(self.scale)
        self.oc = self.ConvolutionTranspose.weight.data.size(0)
        self.tensor_size = tensor_size[0], out_channels, (tensor_size[2] - 1
            ) * strides[0] - 2 * padding[0] + filter_size[0], (tensor_size[
            3] - 1) * strides[1] - 2 * padding[1] + filter_size[1]
        if not pre_nm:
            if normalization is not None:
                self.Normalization = Normalizations((self.tensor_size[0], 
                    out_channels * pst_expansion, self.tensor_size[2], self
                    .tensor_size[3]), normalization, **kwargs)
            if activation in ['relu', 'relu6', 'lklu', 'tanh', 'sigm',
                'maxo', 'rmxo', 'swish']:
                self.Activation = Activations(activation)
        self.pre_nm = pre_nm

    def forward(self, tensor, output_size=None):
        if hasattr(self, 'dropout'):
            tensor = self.dropout(tensor)
        if self.pre_nm:
            if hasattr(self, 'Normalization'):
                tensor = self.Normalization(tensor)
            if hasattr(self, 'Activation'):
                tensor = self.Activation(tensor)
            if output_size is None:
                output_size = self.tensor_size
            output_size = tensor.size(0), self.oc, output_size[2], output_size[
                3]
            tensor = self.ConvolutionTranspose(tensor, output_size=output_size)
            if self.equalized:
                tensor = tensor.mul(self.scale)
        else:
            if output_size is None:
                output_size = self.tensor_size
            output_size = tensor.size(0), self.oc, output_size[2], output_size[
                3]
            tensor = self.ConvolutionTranspose(tensor, output_size=output_size)
            if self.equalized:
                tensor = tensor.mul(self.scale)
            if hasattr(self, 'Normalization'):
                tensor = self.Normalization(tensor)
            if hasattr(self, 'Activation'):
                tensor = self.Activation(tensor)
        return tensor

<#sep#> is 0.25 / sqrt(43/8) = (1/4) * sqrt(8/43) = sqrt(8)/ (4*sqrt(43)) ) = 2*sqrt(2)/(4*sqrt(43)) ) = sqrt(2)/(2*sqrt(43)) ) = sqrt(86)/86 as before.

So this simplifies to sqrt(86)/86. Therefore, the minimal distance is sqrt(86)/86.

But wait, sqrt(86) is approx 9.273, so 9.273/86 ≈ 0.1078. Given that it's a unit cube, this seems plausible. But let me check if there's an alternative way to compute this.

Alternatively, perhaps using vectors, but another approach might involve parametrizing the lines and finding the minimum distance.

Let me attempt that as a verification.

The parametric equations:

Line EC: (t, t, 1 - t) for t ∈ [0,1]

Line HK: (s, 1 - 0.75s, 1 - 0.75s) for s ∈ [0,1]

The distance squared between a point on EC and a point on HK is:

(t - s)^2 + (t - (1 - 0.75s))^2 + ( (1 - t) - (1 - 0.75s) )^2

Simplify this expression.

First, compute each component:

x: t - s

y: t - 1 + 0.75s

z: 1 - t - 1 + 0.75s = -t + 0.75s

So distance squared is:

(t - s)^2 + (t - 1 + 0.75s)^2 + (-t + 0.75s)^2

Let me denote this as D^2(t, s) = (t - s)^2 + (t - 1 + 0.75s)^2 + (-t + 0.75s)^2

To find the minimal distance, we can take partial derivatives with respect to t and s, set them to zero, and solve.

Compute ∂D²/∂t and ∂D²/∂s.

First, expand D^2(t, s):

= (t - s)^2 + (t - 1 + 0.75s)^2 + (-t + 0.75s)^2

Expand each term:

First term: (t - s)^2 = t² - 2ts + s²

Second term: (t -1 + 0.75s)^2 = (t + 0.75s - 1)^2 = t² + (0.75s)^2 + 1 + 2*t*(0.75s) - 2*t*1 - 2*(0.75s)*1

= t² + 0.5625s² + 1 + 1.5ts - 2t - 1.5s

Third term: (-t + 0.75s)^2 = t² - 1.5ts + 0.5625s²

So adding all three terms:

First term: t² - 2ts + s²

Second term: t² + 0.5625s² + 1 + 1.5ts - 2t - 1.5s

Third term: t² - 1.5ts + 0.5625s²

Total D²:

t² -2ts + s² + t² + 0.5625s² +1 +1.5ts -2t -1.5s + t² -1.5ts + 0.5625s²

Combine like terms:

t² + t² + t² = 3t²

-2ts +1.5ts -1.5ts = (-2 +1.5 -1.5)ts = (-2)ts

Now the key challenge is implementing the helper function that checks whether a grid can be tiled with a tile's transformations.

How can this helper be done?

Idea for helper:

The function 'can_tile_grid(grid, tile_variants)' → returns true if the grid can be tiled with transformed copies of the tile using any of the variants (rotated etc).

Approach:

The tiles must cover every filled cell exactly once.

The easiest way might be a backtracking approach: 

We iterate over every empty cell (or start at the first filled cell), then try to place a tile (in any orientation) over that cell such that it covers exactly some of the tiles and doesn't overlap previous placements. 

Repeat until covered fully.

This approach would work for small grids, but could be time-consuming.

An optimizing approach may be needed, but given the problem's examples, it might be manageable.

Steps:

To implement the 'can Tile function' for a grid and a list of tile variants:

The grid is represented as a list of lists (for example), and the tile variants are each a set of (dx, dy) coordinates relative to their anchor point (e.g., the top-left corner of their canonical form).

The function can proceed as follows:

Mark the grid's coordinates as covered.

Pick the first cell in the grid (sorted row-wise) that is part of the shape (i.e., a filled square) and not yet covered.

For that cell (x,y), we try placing one of the tile variants in such way that the top-left corner is at (x,y). 

Then, for each variant j (e.g., 8 possible orientations):

   Determine the tile's relative coordinates. The top left corner is at (x, y), so the tile's offsets (deltas) are extended from there.

   So the tile's cells' positions would be:

      (x + dx_i, y + dy_i) for each (dx_i, dy_i) in the variant's coordinates.

   Now check that all these positions:

      Are within the bounds of the grid.

      Are part of the shape (as per the original).

      Are currently not marked as covered.

   If that's true, then we can tentatively place the tile:

      Mark these cells as covered.

      Proceed recursively to cover the remaining cells.

      If the recursive placement covers everything, then return True.

      (backtrack).

If any one of these possible placements leads to a full covering → return True.

Else, continue with the other options.

This would be a backtracking search with pruning.

This can work for small grids (like the examples given) but would be too slow for large grids.

But since the problem's test cases are small (as per examples), it's possible this would work in a programming competition setting.

So, to implement this:

The helper function can look like this:

def can_tiling(grid, tile_variants, original_shape, covered):

    # grid is the current state? Or original?

- The current covered set (a 2D grid of booleans).

The function is called recursively.

Another point that is important is canonic form of the tiles:

Suppose we have a tile region in the first shape. To represent it as canonical, we can represent it with its cells shifted so that the minimum x and y are 0.

For example:

Take the points of the tile relative to their bottom-most then left-most point.

Example:

Tile cell positions at (1,2), (1,3), (2,2) → subtract (1,2) to get (0,0), (0,1), (1,0).

This gives a canonical version.

Thus, any rotation/reflection would be converted into their own canonical form, but their positions can be compared via rotation.

But when generating all possible rotations and reflections, perhaps it's better to store for each tile variant the possible orientations and then generate their canonical forms.

Wait, for each variant, we can express it as a set of coordinates relative to its own origin (like the top-left point).

So for a given variant, when trying to place it at (x,y), the variant's origin is at (x,y), and the other cells are offset by their relative coordinates.

Thus, for each variant, when generating its coordinates, the coordinates are the positions relative to the origin.

To generate all rotations and reflections:

The original canonical form can be transformed by rotating 90 degrees, flipping, etc. each rotation or reflection creates a new variant's relative coordinates.

Thus, for a canonical form, the list of all transformations (rotations and reflections) can be generated and stored.

Thus, given a canonical tile form, we can generate 8 possible orientations (rotations and reflections).

The code for generating the rotated variants would involve:

Function to generate all eight transformations for regular polygon rotations and reflections:

- identity 

- rotations by 90, 180, 270 degrees.

- reflections over vertical, horizontal, and both diagonals.

Wait for polyomino tiles, the number of possible orientations (for square grids) is limited to rotations (0,90,180,270) and reflections.

Thus, every tile has at most 8 variants.

Thus, for each canonical form, we can precompute all 8 transformed versions.

Thus, code-wise:

def generate_transformations(canonical_shape):

   transformations = []

   # The canonical shape is list of (dx, dy) relative to top-left (0,0).

   # To rotate and reflect.

   # First generate rotations.

   variants = []

   # Rotation by 90 degrees (counter-clockwise about (0,0)).

   def rotate_90(coords):

            new_coords = []

            for (dx, dy) in coords:

                new_dx = -dy

                new_dy = dx

                new_coords.append( (new_dx, new_dy) )

            return new_coords

    def reflect(coords):

            new_coords = [ (-dx, dy) for (dx, dy) in coords ]

            return new_coords

    # For the original shape:

    # Need to consider rotations and reflections.

    # Maybe better to generate all 8 variations.

   variants = []

   current = canonical_shape

   # Start with the original and rotate 4 times (0,90,180, 270).

   for _ in range(4):

       variants.append(current)

       # Reflect.

       reflected = reflect(current)

       variants.append(reflected)

       # Now rotate current to generate next variant.

       current = rotate_90(current)

   Wait, this might not collect all possibilities, but need to collect all eight possible orientations.

import math
import torch
from torch.nn.modules.utils import _pair
import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.utils.data
from torch.nn.parameter import Parameter
from torch.nn.functional import pad
from torch.nn.modules import Module


def conv2d_same_padding(input, weight, bias=None, stride=1, padding=1,
    dilation=1, groups=1):
    input_rows = input.size(2)
    filter_rows = weight.size(2)
    (filter_rows - 1) * dilation[0] + 1
    out_rows = (input_rows + stride[0] - 1) // stride[0]
    padding_rows = max(0, (out_rows - 1) * stride[0] + (filter_rows - 1) *
        dilation[0] + 1 - input_rows)
    rows_odd = padding_rows % 2 != 0
    padding_cols = max(0, (out_rows - 1) * stride[0] + (filter_rows - 1) *
        dilation[0] + 1 - input_rows)
    cols_odd = padding_rows % 2 != 0
    if rows_odd or cols_odd:
        input = pad(input, [0, int(cols_odd), 0, int(rows_odd)])
    return F.conv2d(input, weight, bias, stride, padding=(padding_rows // 2,
        padding_cols // 2), dilation=dilation, groups=groups)


class _ConvNd(Module):

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size, stride,
        padding, dilation, transposed, output_padding, groups, bias,
        weight_=None):
        super(_ConvNd, self).__init__()
        if in_channels % groups != 0:
            raise ValueError('in_channels must be divisible by groups')
        if out_channels % groups != 0:
            raise ValueError('out_channels must be divisible by groups')
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.stride = stride
        self.padding = padding
        self.dilation = dilation
        self.transposed = transposed
        self.output_padding = output_padding
        self.groups = groups
        if transposed:
            self.weight = Parameter(torch.Tensor(in_channels, out_channels //
                groups, *kernel_size))
        elif weight_ is None:
            self.weight = Parameter(torch.Tensor(out_channels, in_channels //
                groups, *kernel_size))
        elif weight_ is not None and isinstance(weight_, torch.Tensor):
            self.weight = Parameter(weight_, requires_grad=False)
        else:
            raise ValueError('不支持的类型！')
        if bias:
            self.bias = Parameter(torch.Tensor(out_channels))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('bias', None)
        self.reset_parameters()

    def reset_parameters(self):
        n = self.in_channels
        for k in self.kernel_size:
            n *= k
        stdv = 1.0 / math.sqrt(n)
        self.weight.data.uniform_(-stdv, stdv)
        if self.bias is not None:
            self.bias.data.uniform_(-stdv, stdv)

    def __repr__(self):
        s = (
            '{name}({in_channels}, {out_channels}, kernel_size={kernel_size}, stride={stride}'
            )
        if self.padding != (0,) * len(self.padding):
            s += ', padding={padding}'
        if self.dilation != (1,) * len(self.dilation):
            s += ', dilation={dilation}'
        if self.output_padding != (0,) * len(self.output_padding):
            s += ', output_padding={output_padding}'
        if self.groups != 1:
            s += ', groups={groups}'
        if self.bias is None:
            s += ', bias=False'
        s += ')'
        return s.format(name=self.__class__.__name__, **self.__dict__)


class Conv2d(_ConvNd):

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size, stride=1,
        padding=0, dilation=1, groups=1, bias=True, weight_=None):
        kernel_size = _pair(kernel_size)
        stride = _pair(stride)
        padding = _pair(padding)
        dilation = _pair(dilation)
        super(Conv2d, self).__init__(in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size,
            stride, padding, dilation, False, _pair(0), groups, bias,
            weight_=weight_)

    def forward(self, input):
        return conv2d_same_padding(input, self.weight, self.bias, self.
            stride, self.padding, self.dilation, self.groups)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'out_channels': 4, 'kernel_size': 4}]

The shortest distance between two skew lines can be found using the formula:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|\overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot (v \times w)|}{|v \times w|}
\]
where \(\overrightarrow{PQ}\) is the vector connecting a point on the first line to a point on the second line, \(v\) and \(w\) are the direction vectors of the lines, and \(v \times w\) is the cross product of the direction vectors.

First, let's think of generate contiguous regions.

Generating all contiguous regions of a certain size within a grid.

This can be done with BFS or DFS.

We can implement this via a recursive backtracking approach:

We can try to build all possible regions starting from each cell, expanding in all directions, and accumulate all unique regions.

To not duplicate the same region that can be shifted, a canonical form is needed. 

但是 to生成 all contiguous regions of size s in the first shape, it is necessary to find all such parts.

   use a recursive search to expand the region, adding adjacent cells until size 到达 s.

   meanwhile, store the current region's coordinates in canonical form.

 To avoid duplication (如， the same region's different shifts→ but canonical forms handle that):

 But even with canonical forms, the same region may be generated multiple times from different starting points.

 Thus this requires storing all canonical forms, and ensuring uniqueness.

Alternatively, we can represent each region's canonical form and store only unique ones.

 The steps are:

1. Initialize a set to hold all regions' canonical forms.

2. Iterate over all cells in the first shape.

3. For each cell, perform a BFS or DFS to generate all possible regions of size s including that cell as part of the region.

Phase<#sep#>import collections
import torch
import warnings
from typing import Optional
from typing import Union
from typing import Any
from typing import Callable
from typing import Tuple
import torch.nn
from torch.nn.modules.loss import _Loss
from enum import Enum
import collections.abc


def issequenceiterable(obj: 'Any') ->bool:
    """
    Determine if the object is an iterable sequence and is not a string.
    """
    if torch.is_tensor(obj):
        return int(obj.dim()) > 0
    return isinstance(obj, collections.abc.Iterable) and not isinstance(obj,
        str)


def ensure_tuple(vals: 'Any') ->Tuple[Any, ...]:
    """
    Returns a tuple of `vals`.
    """
    if not issequenceiterable(vals):
        vals = vals,
    return tuple(vals)


def ensure_tuple_size(tup: 'Any', dim: 'int', pad_val: 'Any'=0) ->Tuple[Any,
    ...]:
    """
    Returns a copy of `tup` with `dim` values by either shortened or padded with `pad_val` as necessary.
    """
    tup = ensure_tuple(tup) + (pad_val,) * dim
    return tuple(tup[:dim])


def one_hot(labels: 'torch.Tensor', num_classes: 'int', dtype:
    'torch.dtype'=torch.float, dim: 'int'=1) ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    For a tensor `labels` of dimensions B1[spatial_dims], return a tensor of dimensions `BN[spatial_dims]`
    for `num_classes` N number of classes.

    Example:

        For every value v = labels[b,1,h,w], the value in the result at [b,v,h,w] will be 1 and all others 0.
        Note that this will include the background label, thus a binary mask should be treated as having 2 classes.
    """
    assert labels.dim() > 0, 'labels should have dim of 1 or more.'
    if labels.ndim < dim + 1:
        shape = ensure_tuple_size(labels.shape, dim + 1, 1)
        labels = labels.reshape(*shape)
    sh = list(labels.shape)
    assert sh[dim
        ] == 1, 'labels should have a channel with length equals to one.'
    sh[dim] = num_classes
    o = torch.zeros(size=sh, dtype=dtype, device=labels.device)
    labels = o.scatter_(dim=dim, index=labels.long(), value=1)
    return labels


class LossReduction(Enum):
    """
    See also:
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.DiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.GeneralizedDiceLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.focal_loss.FocalLoss`
        - :py:class:`monai.losses.tversky.TverskyLoss`
    """
    NONE = 'none'
    MEAN = 'mean'
    SUM = 'sum'


class Weight(Enum):
    """
    See also: :py:class:`monai.losses.dice.GeneralizedDiceLoss`
    """
    SQUARE = 'square'
    SIMPLE = 'simple'
    UNIFORM = 'uniform'


class GeneralizedDiceLoss(_Loss):
    """
    Compute the generalised Dice loss defined in:

        Sudre, C. et. al. (2017) Generalised Dice overlap as a deep learning
        loss function for highly unbalanced segmentations. DLMIA 2017.

    Adapted from:
        https://github.com/NifTK/NiftyNet/blob/v0.6.0/niftynet/layer/loss_segmentation.py#L279
    """

    def __init__(self, include_background: 'bool'=True, to_onehot_y: 'bool'
        =False, sigmoid: 'bool'=False, softmax: 'bool'=False, other_act:
        'Optional[Callable]'=None, w_type: 'Union[Weight, str]'=Weight.
        SQUARE, reduction: 'Union[LossReduction, str]'=LossReduction.MEAN,
        smooth_nr: 'float'=1e-05, smooth_dr: 'float'=1e-05, batch: 'bool'=False
        ) ->None:
        """
        Args:
            include_background: If False channel index 0 (background category) is excluded from the calculation.
            to_onehot_y: whether to convert `y` into the one-hot format. Defaults to False.
            sigmoid: If True, apply a sigmoid function to the prediction.
            softmax: If True, apply a softmax function to the prediction.
            other_act: if don't want to use `sigmoid` or `softmax`, use other callable function to execute
                other activation layers, Defaults to ``None``. for example:
                `other_act = torch.tanh`.
            squared_pred: use squared versions of targets and predictions in the denominator or not.
            w_type: {``"square"``, ``"simple"``, ``"uniform"``}
                Type of function to transform ground truth volume to a weight factor. Defaults to ``"square"``.
            reduction: {``"none"``, ``"mean"``, ``"sum"``}
                Specifies the reduction to apply to the output. Defaults to ``"mean"``.

                - ``"none"``: no reduction will be applied.
                - ``"mean"``: the sum of the output will be divided by the number of elements in the output.
                - ``"sum"``: the output will be summed.
            smooth_nr: a small constant added to the numerator to avoid zero.
            smooth_dr: a small constant added to the denominator to avoid nan.
            batch: whether to sum the intersection and union areas over the batch dimension before the dividing.
                Defaults to False, intersection over union is computed from each item in the batch.

        Raises:
            TypeError: When ``other_act`` is not an ``Optional[Callable]``.
            ValueError: When more than 1 of [``sigmoid=True``, ``softmax=True``, ``other_act is not None``].
                Incompatible values.

        """
        super().__init__(reduction=LossReduction(reduction).value)
        if other_act is not None and not callable(other_act):
            raise TypeError(
                f'other_act must be None or callable but is {type(other_act).__name__}.'
                )
        if int(sigmoid) + int(softmax) + int(other_act is not None) > 1:
            raise ValueError(
                'Incompatible values: more than 1 of [sigmoid=True, softmax=True, other_act is not None].'
                )
        self.include_background = include_background
        self.to_onehot_y = to_onehot_y
        self.sigmoid = sigmoid
        self.softmax = softmax
        self.other_act = other_act
        w_type = Weight(w_type)
        self.w_func: 'Callable' = torch.ones_like
        if w_type == Weight.SIMPLE:
            self.w_func = torch.reciprocal
        elif w_type == Weight.SQUARE:
            self.w_func = lambda x: torch.reciprocal(x * x)
        self.smooth_nr = float(smooth_nr)
        self.smooth_dr = float(smooth_dr)
        self.batch = batch

    def forward(self, input: 'torch.Tensor', target: 'torch.Tensor'
        ) ->torch.Tensor:
        """
        Args:
            input: the shape should be BNH[WD].
            target: the shape should be BNH[WD].

        Raises:
            ValueError: When ``self.reduction`` is not one of ["mean", "sum", "none"].

        """
        if self.sigmoid:
            input = torch.sigmoid(input)
        n_pred_ch = input.shape[1]
        if self.softmax:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `softmax=True` ignored.')
            else:
                input = torch.softmax(input, 1)
        if self.other_act is not None:
            input = self.other_act(input)
        if self.to_onehot_y:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `to_onehot_y=True` ignored.')
            else:
                target = one_hot(target, num_classes=n_pred_ch)
        if not self.include_background:
            if n_pred_ch == 1:
                warnings.warn(
                    'single channel prediction, `include_background=False` ignored.'
                    )
            else:
                target = target[:, 1:]
                input = input[:, 1:]
        assert target.shape == input.shape, f'ground truth has differing shape ({target.shape}) from input ({input.shape})'
        reduce_axis = list(range(2, len(input.shape)))
        if self.batch:
            reduce_axis = [0] + reduce_axis
        intersection = torch.sum(target * input, reduce_axis)
        ground_o = torch.sum(target, reduce_axis)
        pred_o = torch.sum(input, reduce_axis)
        denominator = ground_o + pred<#sep#> product of the direction vectors.

1. Compute the vector \(\overrightarrow{PQ}\) connecting \(E(0,0,1)\) and \(H(0,1,1)\):
   \[
   \overrightarrow{PQ} = (0, 1, 0)
   \]

2. Compute the cross product \(v \times w\) of the direction vectors \(v = (1, 1, -1)\) and \(w = (1, -0.75, -0.75)\):
   \[
   v \times w = \begin{vmatrix}
   \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\
   1 & 1 & -1 \\
   1 & -0.75 & -0.75
   \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i} (1 \cdot -0.75 - (-1) \cdot -0.75) - \mathbf{j} (1 \cdot -0.75 - (-1) \cdot 1) + \mathbf{k} (1 \cdot -0.75 - 1 \cdot -0.75)
   \]
   \[
   v \times w = \mathbf{i} (-0.75 - 0.75) - \mathbf{j} (-0.75 + 1) + \mathbf{k} (-0.75 + 0.75) = (-1.5, -0.25, -1.75)
   \]

3. Calculate the dot product \(\overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot (v \times w)\):
   \[
   \overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot (v \times w) = (0, 1, 0) \cdot (-1.5, -0.25, -1.75) = 0 \cdot (-1.5) + 1 \cdot (-0.25) + 0 \cdot (-1.75) = -0.25
   \]

4. Compute the magnitude of \(v \times w\):
   \[
   |v \times w| = \sqrt{(-1.5)^2 + (-0.25)^2 + (-1.75)^2} = \sqrt{2.25 + 0.0625 + 3.0625} = \sqrt{5.375} = \sqrt{\frac{43}{8}}
   \]

5. The shortest distance is:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{|\overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot (v \times w)|}{|v \times w|} = \frac{|-0.25|}{\sqrt{\frac{43}{8}}} = \frac{0.25}{\sqrt{\frac{43}{8}}} = \frac{0.25 \cdot \sqrt{8}}{\sqrt{43}} = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2\sqrt{43}} = \frac{\sqrt{2} \cdot \sqrt{43}}{2 \cdot 43} = \frac{\sqrt{86}}{86}
   \]

Thus, the shortest distance between the line segments \(EC\) and \(HK\) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{86}}{86}}\).<#sep#>. Initialize a set to hold all regions' canonical forms.

2. Iterate over all cells in the first shape.

3. For each cell, perform a BFS or DFS to generate all possible regions of size s including that cell as part of the region.

 Def generate_all_regions(grid, target_size):

   regions = set()

   visited = a dictionary or some structure to track unique canonical forms.

   for each cell (i, j) in the grid:

       if cell is part of the first shape (marked as filled):

           # start a new BFS to build regions from this cell.

           # Use a queue for BFS, each item is a set of coordinates representing a region.

           queue = deque()

           # 初始 region is just this cell.

           initial_set = { (i,j) }

           canonical_form = normalize_rel(initial_set)

           # 向regions中添加，如果 is not already added.

           if canonical_form not in visited:

               visited.add(canonical_form)

           queue.append( (initial_set, canonical_form) )

           while queue not empty:

               current_set, current_canonical = queue.pop()

               if len(current_set) == target_size:

                   regions.add(current_canonical)

               elif len(current_set) < target_size:

                   # 找 adjacent cells not in current_set that are part of the first shape.

                   for cell in current_set:

                       for direction in neighbors:

                   中, ,

                   for each neighbor cell (ni, nj) not in current_set:

                       if the cell (n_i, n_j) is in first shape:

                           new_set = current_set union { (ni,nj) }

                           new_set as frozenset.

                           new canonical = normalize_rel(new_set)

                           if canonical not in visited:

                               visited.add(new_canonical)

                               queue.append( (new_set, new_canonical ) )

     return regions.

 这种方式會生成 all possible regions， re the unique canonical form.

 but the issue is that the order of adding adjacent cells can lead to duplicates being pruned through the visited set.

 But this approach is computationally expensive for larger regions.

 Another optimization: To normalize the region's coordinates.

 normalize_rel function takes a set of coordinates (in grid coordinates) and translates them so that the minimum row and column are at 0.

 For example, for a set of points like (2,3), (2,4), (3,3):

 the min_row is 2; min_col is 3.

 thus the canonical form is 翻 translate 的 positions by subtracting the minimum coordinates:

 (0, 0), (0,1), (1,0).

 This ensures that regions that are translations are considered the same canonical form.

 Thus, when generating the canonical form, we can compare and remove duplicates.

 this makes the visited set 能 to avoid duplicate regions.

 this approach reduces the set of regions.

 notwithstanding ,  this could work for small regions.

 Thus, this is a possible way forward.

 Once generated all 'regions' canonical forms in the first shape, each of them is for size s_candidate.

                           recursively call this function. if returns true, return true.

            else:
                target = target[:, 1:]
                input = input[:, 1:]
        assert target.shape == input.shape, f'ground truth has differing shape ({target.shape}) from input ({input.shape})'
        reduce_axis = list(range(2, len(input.shape)))
        if self.batch:
            reduce_axis = [0] + reduce_axis
        intersection = torch.sum(target * input, reduce_axis)
        ground_o = torch.sum(target, reduce_axis)
        pred_o = torch.sum(input, reduce_axis)
        denominator = ground_o + pred_o
        w = self.w_func(ground_o.float())
        for b in w:
            infs = torch.isinf(b)
            b[infs] = 0.0
            b[infs] = torch.max(b)
        f: 'torch.Tensor' = 1.0 - (2.0 * (intersection * w).sum(0 if self.
            batch else 1) + self.smooth_nr) / ((denominator * w).sum(0 if
            self.batch else 1) + self.smooth_dr)
        if self.reduction == LossReduction.MEAN.value:
            f = torch.mean(f)
        elif self.reduction == LossReduction.SUM.value:
            f = torch.sum(f)
        elif self.reduction == LossReduction.NONE.value:
            pass
        else:
            raise ValueError(
                f'Unsupported reduction: {self.reduction}, available options are ["mean", "sum", "none"].'
                )
        return f

 Alternative approach 相对 the problem:

 the optimal number of pieces is the minimal number of contiguous partitions needed such that:

 1. the pieces can be rotated etc and placed at the second shape without overlapping, covering all of it.

 Thus, each piece can be in different shapes, but together must form both tiling of first and second.

 The minimal such partition is sought.

 Thus the problem is a partitioning of the first shape into contiguous regions, such that when those are rearranged, they tile the second.

 Thus the optimal partition is the smallest possible set of connected parts.

 这个 problem 의 minimal number is thus the minimal cut to split first into pieces that can be rearranged.

 Thus the way to find this is perhaps to find the minimum over all possible ways to divide into contiguous pieces, and check whether the pieces can be re arranged into the second shape.

 这 is a classic NP-hard problem, so no efficient algorithm exists, but for small cases, it can be attempted with backtracking.

 The 焦点 is thus to generate all possible partitions of the first shape into contiguous regions, 然后 for each partition, check if their pieces can be rotated etc to fit into the second.

对 minimal the number, this is compute from the minimal number across all valid such partitions.

 Thus, the approach would proceed as follows:

1. Generate all possible contiguous partitions of the first shape into K regions, where K is the current minimal candidate (start with 1, check whether possible; if no, check k=2 etc).

2. For each possible partition into k regions,

   a. check if each region can be rotated/reflected (individually)

   b. place the أو rotated pieces into the second shape, such that they cover it without overlapping.

 If such a partition exists, return k.

 thus the minimal such k is the answer.

 This would be a brute-force approach.

此 approach requires the following：

 a.枚举 possible partition  into k regions for smallest possible k.

 The first step (_ generating all partitions into two contiguous regions) is the most challenging part.

 generating all ways to divide the shape into two contiguous regions:

 the approach may be to select a cell to split or find all possible ways to partition the grid, but this is non-trivial.

 an alternative approach is to treat the first shape's grid as a graph, and find all possible cuts between two connected components.

 each partition into two disjoint connected regions.

 The number of such partitions is exponential, but for small grids, this can可能导致 iva in代码.

 for example, for a shape of 5 cells, the number of possible partitions into two may be manageable.

 To generate all possible partitions into two contiguous regions:

 the idea is to iterate over all possible subsets of the first shape's cells that form a contiguous region (for one side of the partition), and the complement is also contiguous.

 thus部长的 the first region can be any contiguous subset such that the compl ment is also contiguous.7

 thus, the algorithm for这 is:

  -(enum3rate all conts regions of sizes from 1到 area-1.

   - for each such region, check if the complement is also contiguous.

   - if yes → this is a valid partition.

 this is feasible for small regions.

 the following steps are:

 。 gen1r all contiguousS reations of the first shape of size 1到 area-1.

 for each such region peC eA; rore; the complement (all other cells notIn the region) 'S cont<#sep#> 31	    :target: https://codeclimate.com/github/12rambau/sepal_ui/maintainability

    66	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows. 
    67	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.

    75	The Conventional Commits specification is a lightweight convention on top of commit messages. It provides an easy set of rules for creating an explicit commit history; which makes it easier to write automated tools on top of. This convention dovetails with SemVer, by describing the features, fixes, and breaking changes made in commit messages.

    83	
    84	-   the modification you<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


class AngularPenaltySMLoss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_features, out_features, loss_type='arcface', eps=
        1e-07, s=None, m=None):
        """
        Angular Penalty Softmax Loss

        Three 'loss_types' available: ['arcface', 'sphereface', 'cosface']
        These losses are described in the following papers: 
        
        ArcFace: https://arxiv.org/abs/1801.07698
        SphereFace: https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.08063
        CosFace/Ad Margin: https://arxiv.org/abs/1801.05599

        """
        super(AngularPenaltySMLoss, self).__init__()
        loss_type = loss_type.lower()
        assert loss_type in ['arcface', 'sphereface', 'cosface']
        if loss_type == 'arcface':
            self.s = 64.0 if not s else s
            self.m = 0.5 if not m else m
        if loss_type == 'sphereface':
            self.s = 64.0 if not s else s
            self.m = 1.35 if not m else m
        if loss_type == 'cosface':
            self.s = 30.0 if not s else s
            self.m = 0.4 if not m else m
        self.loss_type = loss_type
        self.in_features = in_features
        self.out_features = out_features
        self.fc = nn.Linear(in_features, out_features, bias=False)
        self.eps = eps

    def forward(self, x, labels):
        """
        input shape (N, in_features)
        """
        assert len(x) == len(labels)
        assert torch.min(labels) >= 0
        assert torch.max(labels) < self.out_features
        for W in self.fc.parameters():
            W = F.normalize(W, p=2, dim=1)
        x = F.normalize(x, p=2, dim=1)
        wf = self.fc(x)
        if self.loss_type == 'cosface':
            numerator = self.s * (torch.diagonal(wf.transpose(0, 1)[labels]
                ) - self.m)
        if self.loss_type == 'arcface':
            numerator = self.s * torch.cos(torch.acos(torch.clamp(torch.
                diagonal(wf.transpose(0, 1)[labels]), -1.0 + self.eps, 1 -
                self.eps)) + self.m)
        if self.loss_type == 'sphereface':
            numerator = self.s * torch.cos(self.m * torch.acos(torch.clamp(
                torch.diagonal(wf.transpose(0, 1)[labels]), -1.0 + self.eps,
                1 - self.eps)))
        excl = torch.cat([torch.cat((wf[i, :y], wf[i, y + 1:])).unsqueeze(0
            ) for i, y in enumerate(labels)], dim=0)
        denominator = torch.exp(numerator) + torch.sum(torch.exp(self.s *
            excl), dim=1)
        L = numerator - torch.log(denominator)
        return -torch.mean(L)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.ones([4], dtype=torch.int64)]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_features': 4, 'out_features': 4}]

    76	
    77	You can learn more about Conventional Commits following this `link <https://www.conventionalcommits.org/en/v1.0.0/>`_
    78	
    79	What can I push and where
    80	-------------------------
    81	
    82	Our branching system embed some rules to avoid crash of the production environment. If you want to contribute to this framework, here are some basic rules that we try our best to follow :
    83	
    84	-   the modification you offer is solving a critical bug in prod : **PR in hotfix**
    85	-   the modification you propose solve the following issues : test, documentation, typo, quality, refactoring, translation **PR in master**
    86	-   the modification you propose is a new feature : open an issue to discuss with the maintainers and then **PR to develop**
    87	
    88	the maintainers will try their best to use PR for new features, to help the community follow the development, for other modification they will simply push to the appropriate branch
    89	
    90	Create a new release
    91	--------------------
    92	
    93	.. danger:: 
    94	
    95	    for maintainers only 
    96	    
    97	 .. warning::
    98	 
    99	     You need to use the :code:`commitizen` lib to create your release: `<https://commitizen-tools.github.io/commitizen>`_
   100	    
   101	In the files change the version number by runnning commitizen `bump`: 
   102	
   103	.. code-block:: console
   104	
   105	    cz bump
   106	
   107	It should modify for you the version number in :code:`sepal_ui/__init__.py`, :code:`setup.py`, and :code:`.cz.yaml` according to sementic versionning thanks to the conventional commit that we use in the lib. 
   108	
   109	It will also update the :code:`CHANGELOG.md` file with the latest commits, sorted by categories if you run the following code, using the version bumped in the previous commit.
   110	
   111	.. code-block:: console 
   112	
   113	    cz changelog --unreleased-version="v_x.x.x"
   114	
   115	Then push the current :code:`master` branch to the :code:`release` branch. You can now create a new tag with your new version number. use the same convention as the one found in :code:`.cz.yaml`: :code:`v_$minor.$major.$patch$prerelease`.
   116	
   117	.. warning::
   118	
   119	    The target branch of the new release is :code:`release` not :code:`master`. 
   120	    
   121	The CI should take everything in control from here and execute the :code:`Upload Python Package` GitHub Action that is publishing the new version on `PyPi <https://badge.fury.io/py/sepal-ui>`_.
   122	    
   123	Once it's done you need to trigger the rebuild of SEPAL. modify the following `file <https://github.com/openforis/sepal/blob/master/modules/sandbox/docker/script/init_sepal_ui.sh>`_ with the latest version number and the rebuild will start automatically. 
   124	
   125	
   126	Setting ENV variables
   127	---------------------
   128	
   129	Sometimes is useful to create enviromental variables to store some data that your workflows will receive (i.e. component testing). For example, to perform the local tests of the :code:`planetapi` sepal module, the :code:`PLANET_API_KEY` and :code:`PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS` env vars are required, even though they are also skippable.
   130	
   131	To store a variable in your local session, just type :code:`export=` followed by the var value.
   132	
   133	.. code-block:: console 
   134	
   135	    $ export PLANET_API_KEY="neverending_resourcesapi"
   136	    
   137	However, this variable will expire everytime you start a new session, to create it every session and make it live longer, go to your :code:`home` folder and save the previous line in the :code:`.bash_profile` file.
   138	    
   139	.. code-block:: console 
   140	
   141	    $ vim .bash_profile
   142	
   143	.. note::
   144	    The current enviromental keys and its structure is the following:
   145	
   146	    - PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS: '{"username": "user@neim.com", "password": "secure"}'
   147	    - PLANET_API_KEY: "string_planet_api_key"
   148

   150	
   151	    pre
